It was excluded because it was told in Tamil and showed southerners defeating northerners. Should Dravidians still hesitate to demand a place of their own? Evidence records the victories of Tirumavalavan, Karikala Valavan, Nedunchezhiyan who crossed the Aryan army, Perunchotru Udiyan Cheralathan, Imayavaramban Neduncheralathan and others. One may even wonder whether Cheralathan's generosity in feeding northern Aryans gave them a taste of Tamil wealth and encouraged them to enter the south. Should liberality destroy Tamil life? Nor have we forgotten Rajaraja Chola, Rajendra's invasion of Burma or the history of Gangaikonda Chola. These were battlefield victories won beneath many spears.

Aryan association did not render Tamil victory unworthy of history; it rendered Tamils intellectually unworthy of claiming their place. Ninety-five in a hundred Tamils became uneducated while Aryans were learned. A people once filled with an unprejudiced mind, love, valour and compassion pawned reason in god's name and submitted to fate.

Tamils who once met sword with sword, shielded thrusts, tore out embedded spears and killed opponents laughing without fear later knelt before the army of the almanac, believed the chanting priestly crowd and bowed to darbha grass.

The artistic net of Aryanism also causes grief. Enchanted by painting, sculpture, epic structure, literary course, verbal sweetness and nuance, Tamils fail to see religion baited with art. The *Kamba Ramayanam* and *Periyapuranam* feed religious doctrine under literature's name, becoming texts of Aryan servitude purchased by Tamils as poison that kills awareness. Even the *Purananuru* contains Aryan intrusion: alongside Nedunchezhiyan's victory over an Aryan army stands a poem on Palyagasalai Mudukudumi Peruvaluthi and praise of the sacrifice of the Brahmin Kauniyan of Poonchatru.

The *Silappathikaram*, in the *Nadukal Kathai* (Canto 28), says Senguttuvan accepted the Brahmin Matalan's word and performed a sacrifice. Since sacrifice belonged to and sustained Aryans, how can one deny that the Chera defeated Aryans in the north yet lived subject to Aryanism in Dravidian country? Only self-awareness can release Tamils from the trance of slavery and restore self-rule.

The Pallavas ruled from Kanchipuram between roughly 300 and 700 CE. They supported Sanskrit and the four Vedas, invited Brahmins from the north in large groups, settled them throughout Tamilakam, and granted tax-free villages under names such as Brahmadeya and Vedadanam. Hence many Tamil regions appear today as Chaturvedimangalams.

Telugu history shows the same process earlier. The group entering Tamil country in the fourth century had entered Andhra country in the second. The Ikshvaku and Satavahana dynasties sought higher caste status by taking refuge in Aryanism. When the Ikshvaku ruler Santamula the Great reigned from 200 to 218 CE, he invited Brahmins from the north, made numerous gifts, conducted sacrifices and turned Dravidian country into Aryan ground. B. V. Krishna Rao's *Early Dynasties of Andhra Desa* states:

“His reign was responsible for a greatwave of immigration particularly of Brahmana settlers on Andhra desa from the north and north west. The immigrants came in all probability, at the invitation of the Emperor Santamula, who after a lapse of more...”

## Part 6

“...than a century revived the Vedic sacrifices and particularly the celebrated Vajabeya and Aswamedha..... Thus new villages were founded and Brahmana settlements established.”

During his reign, Mahasantamula's son Virapurushadatha (218–239 CE) recognised the harm that the Aryan incursion had brought to his country and attempted to remove it. Not only did he embrace Buddhism, but, to display his abhorrence of Brahmanism, he had several stone figures carved and installed upon the viewing wall of the Buddhist images erected in his capital. They appear to kick down a linga, with their heels placed upon its summit. Two large images of this kind are photographed in the work cited above and remain visible today.

“The Ikshavahu king who was till than a follower of vedic Brahmanism and a worshipper of Siva in the from of linga re-nounced his faith and became a true convert to Budhism. (p.58)

“In this pannel there is a representation of a monarch crushing with his right heel, a stone linga, which is encircled by many headed serpent. The linga and the serpent apparently symbolise Brahmanism and the worship of Mahesvara. The Brahmanism is denounced by the king in the presence of his ministers and high dignitaries of State.............

“It is proable that ‘Sri Virapurushadatha’ attempted to crush the tide of Brahmanism which recieved great impetus and revival under the aegis of his illustrious father Santhamula the great, only. two decades ago.” (page 59–60)

Thus even in Andhra the Aryan invasion succeeded only around 200 CE, and it is evident that resistance confronted it even then. Andhra history supplies much evidence for the separation of Dravidian country. Our Andhra comrades must soon reach a state in which they understand this.

Quite apart from the many superstitions that have entered literature, multiplying falsehood and ruining people's thought, what is the condition of grammar, the proper domain of learned poets themselves? The *Tolkappiyam* contains some interpolations; the *Virasoliyam* and *Nannul* follow Sanskrit grammar. Their commentaries became comparative grammars of Tamil and Sanskrit even before the discipline of comparative linguistics arose. Yet in later grammar Tamil declined so far that Swaminatha Desikar of the *Ilakkana Kottu* could say, “People would be ashamed even to declare that a language exists with five letters.” Why did this happen, if not because scholars immersed themselves in Sanskrit grammar and neglected Tamil grammar?

Imagination may assist literature, but does it not injure grammar? Let scholars consider how deeply the later work *Venba Pattiyal* (*Vacchanandi Malai*) is sunk in fantasy and how discordant its ideas are. Why should this manuscript, ignorance embodied, be made a university textbook? Can studying it yield any benefit? The *Kapilar Akaval* thunders at Aryans, “You planted four kinds of caste in this country.” Despite knowing that truth, must there be four castes even among letters?

It had been understood and accepted for thousands upon thousands of years that vowels, consonants, compound letters and dependent letters are the classes of letters. Contrary to that truth, Aryanism entered and flourished. Aryan poets undertook to learn Tamil and compose literature, and gradually turned every Aryan poet's mind into a monastery inhabited by Aryan ideas, so that the sound and meaning they uttered expressed Aryanism. Even in grammar, that scientific institution which enables language to function in all three periods of time, the venomous serpent of Aryanism raises its hood and dances!

The *Pattiyal* calls the twelve vowels and first six consonants the Brahmin varna; the next six consonants the royal varna; four consonants the Vaishya varna; and the remaining two the Sudra varna. Fearing perhaps that the Aryan division into Brahmin, Kshatriya, Vaishya and Sudra might one day be abolished among people, they assigned varna correspondences even to the metres and letters used by poets regarded as the country's learned people. The four letters “ல, வ, ற, ன” are said to be Vaishya letters; “ழ” and “ள” are Sudra letters.

One truth emerges even here. Of the three letters “ழ, ற, ன” distinctive to Tamil, all were assigned to the Vaishya or Sudra classes. Although Sanskrit “ல” and “ள” may be pronounced in corresponding ways, the distinctively Tamil “ற” and “ழ” do not exist in Sanskrit. What does it mean to call these Sudra letters?

Is it not the consequence of Aryans having designated the Dravidians—who lived separately enough to be distinguished from Aryans—as “Sudras” (children of a slave woman), members of the fourth varna? Can scholars who wish to abolish such arrogance permit this grammar to remain in force?

Brahmins, it is said, must be praised in *venba*; kings in *asiriyappa*; merchants in *kalippa*; and Sudras in *vanjippa*. This has no basis in literary usage, yet it has entered grammar as a rule.

The excellent and radiant *venba*, deemed foremost among metres, goes to Brahmins; the next-ranking *asiriyappa* goes to kings whose teachers were Brahmins; the less varied *kali* goes to Vaishyas; and *vanjippa*, as if Aryan deception itself had assumed poetic form, goes to Dravidians called Sudras! Must Aryanism receive the first ceremonial betel even when Tamil verse is composed? Even today the *Pattiyal* rules that a *kalambakam*—a composition mixing different metres—must contain 100 verses for gods, 95 for Brahmins, 90 for kings, 70 for ministers, 50 for merchants and 30 for everyone else. In a country where the king himself was regarded as God's incarnation, deceitful writers declared the priestly Brahmin superior to the king and prescribed more verses for him. It is no surprise that such writers spoke in literature or grammar in ways that degraded other people. What grieves us is that scholars continue to uphold these things.

In what respect does the *Pattiyal* differ from Manu's law? Unless it is burned before the people, can Dravidian ethnic awareness and the foundational principle that all living beings are equal by birth be restored? The day when Aryan doctrines, even when they take the form of Tamil books, are offered up to the very fire-feeding sacrificial method practised by Aryans will be the day we recognise ourselves.

Moreover, composing Tamil verse after observing every correspondence prescribed by the *Pattiyal* is a fruitless exercise. All its correspondences—auspiciousness, initial word, assignment of letters, gender, food, varna, day, destiny and *ganam*—restrict the ability to use all the excellent words in Tamil and compose poetry rich in beautiful thought. Since these correspondences prohibit the use of many words, they also created conditions in which poets employed many Sanskrit words without treating them as foreign. The *Pattiyal* can serve only to make pure Tamil, an independent classical language of the highest order, lose its character or to display the condition in which that character has been lost. No one can deny that it is neither suited nor appropriate to Tamil in any way.

Self-awareness is indispensable if Aryanism is to disappear; for self-awareness to grow, the Tamil field must be put in order. Whether in the garden of literature or the cultivated field of grammar, Dravidians who recognise the harm of the Aryan weed will not hesitate to destroy it, even with fire. Therefore, comrades, let us students recognise our duty and strive in every possible way for the Dravidian people to rise and wear the garland of victory.

About the coming of Aryans to India, a researcher named A. L. Saunders, C.S.I.I.C.S., wrote approximately twenty years ago in a study called *The Religion of Indians*:

“About 5,000 years ago, India's climate was somewhat milder than it is now. Almost all the people of India were Dravidians, though they lived in many groups scattered from place to place. They were dark-skinned and somewhat short. As now, they were capable of hard work, very energetic and cheerful, good-natured and free from care. The Aryan invasion disrupted the condition of such people. Under Aryan dominance they were made a servile caste. Because their own country lacked grasslands and because Mongolians drove them out, Aryans came in small groups solely to find a place in which to make a living.

“When they arrived, Dravidians in India lived in many divisions, scattered separately, each exercising authority over a small region. Consequently, disputes and fighting occurred among them.

“The Aryans who had come here to survive exploited this condition. They entered the internal conflicts, joined opposing factions individually, enlarged the strife and wars, and caused the local groups to destroy and exhaust one another.

“The first territories under Aryan control were in the north-western region. From there Aryans continued to persecute, subdue and conquer Dravidians. They did not come as a single group and conquer India in one stroke. At first they arrived as separate bands at different times, fought separately in different places and secured separate victories.

“To describe the way they first arrived: they came like spies, suddenly entered and attacked, seized whatever came to hand and then sought permanent dominance.

“Through warfare, killing Dravidians and enslaving many, they took possession of elevated places and of places possessing every convenience and prominence.

“Once they had obtained enough of these places, they settled here and began spreading throughout the country their arts and customs, and doctrines suited to their own comfort and superiority. We do not possess enough history to describe the original character and history of the Aryans who came here. Nevertheless, their character, religion and language may be inferred to some degree from the Vedas and other sources.

“Priestcraft and ritual were their principal religious concerns. The five elements, Indra and others were their gods. (This resembled conditions in ancient European lands such as Rome and Greece.)

“Rama and Krishna, who today appear as Aryan gods, were warriors who worked to establish Aryan dominance. Rama conquered Dravidians and established Aryan authority in South India and Lanka. Though Aryan priests were not warriors, they were exceedingly powerful.”

The account continues: “Many people made all kinds of efforts against such an Aryan religion, but those efforts proved fruitless. Aryans destroyed Buddhism because they regarded it as hostile to themselves. They also destroyed the endeavours and doctrines of Christ, Muhammad, Raja Ram Mohan Roy and others who established religions without caste distinction and forms of worship and conceptions of God free from superstition. They degraded those religions and made their adherents objects of hatred. Aryans made themselves the masters, gurus, priests, officiants and high castes of human society, preventing the rest of society from raising its head.”

At six in the evening on 21 April 1939, a public meeting sponsored by the South Indian Brahmo Samaj was held in the Samaj building on Anna Pillai Street, Madras.

Scholar S. Somasundara Bharathi, M.A., B.L., presided and delivered an illuminating lecture of approximately one and a half hours on “Ancient Tamil Marriage Customs.” A summary follows:

“Tamil sisters and brothers! Without regard to difference of caste or religion, I call everyone whose mother tongue is Tamil a Tamil. When I came to Madras, I did not expect to speak here. I accepted only because I could not refuse the friends who suddenly asked me to speak.

“What period do we call the age of ancient Tamils? It was the period before Aryan dominance could be imposed upon Tamils—the second and third centuries CE, when some Tamil words began to enter Aryan speech and some Aryan words began to enter Tamil. Aryan dominance did not exist in Tamil country then. Only later, in the third century, did some Aryan words come to be mixed into Tamil: that is, after the period preceding the *Silappathikaram*. Indeed, the *Silappathikaram* itself already displays Aryan admixture. What is discussed here, therefore, is the marriage system of the ancient Tamils before the *Silappathikaram*.

“The very foundations of Aryan and Tamil civilisation concerning family are different. Today the Aryan system has become decisive in family custom. Aryans say it is good for children to divide their father's wealth. That practice has now reached us too. A joint-family system was necessary to Aryans. Why? Aryans were foreigners who came and settled here. The place in which they settled differed completely from their customs. Wherever they went, it was their practice to live alongside the people of that country while considering themselves a separate divine birth.

“Consider an example. Aryans in Germany hate Jews and, in their pride at being Aryan, drive them away. This arrogance belongs to all Aryans.

“Because of this pride, other people in turn hated—and continue to hate—Aryans. When these Aryans settled in North India, they began to regard the northern inhabitants there as inferior. Those inhabitants, being an ignorant and lowly people, began to kick the Aryans who disparaged them. Self-defence consequently became necessary to the Aryans. The *Ramayana* says in many places that asuras kicked them. What does this mean? Because the Aryans' arrogance led them to treat others contemptuously, those others—the asuras—gave them a proper kicking. That is the truth. It is not I but history that says so.

“These Aryans kept watch night and day to protect themselves. They required great influence and protection to fight their enemies without rest. For this reason they greatly desired male children and even regarded the birth of girls as a sin. Thus it was for protection that they had to unite and live collectively.

“Some fools say that Tamils came here and settled through the north-western and north-eastern passes. That is wrong. Tamils have lived in Tamil country since the world arose. Are our Tamils not descended from the ancient people who ‘appeared before stone arose and earth came into being’? Although Tamils fought minor internal battles for political reasons, they were not attacked by foreign peoples. Why? Nature itself gave them the sea on three sides and mountains on the fourth as a fortress. The Tamil therefore desired the distinction of living independently without needing collective defence.

“From the beginning, keeping a separate household was the Tamil custom. If a man desired his father's wealth, both his mother and wife would despise him. The Tamil crossed the rolling sea in search of wealth. His wife would say, ‘Did you earn wealth through your own effort? I do not want an idler who wishes to live on his father's property.’ Once two lovers had joined in thought, it was the Tamil custom for the man to go and acquire wealth before they married.

“Look at Kovalan. He was the only son of a great millionaire. Why did he have to leave at night without anyone's knowledge and go to Madurai? To seek wealth. Would his father not have given it if asked? Or had Kannaki quarrelled with her parents-in-law? At one point Kannaki tells her friend, ‘Do not tell my mother-in-law that I wept. If you do, she will become angry with my lord and ask: “Are you even a man, when you do not know how to provide what your wife needs and keep her without a troubled face?”’ This shows that Kannaki had no quarrel with her mother-in-law; they lived lovingly. Why, then, did Kovalan go to Madurai? Because he thought the world would reproach him for living on property given by his father.

“Moreover, immediately after Kovalan married, their parents established the couple in a separate household. Why? So that they might be seen to gain distinction through separate prosperity. Tamil people therefore considered it disgraceful to live on a father's money. If the father died and left something behind, it belonged to the children. But there was no quarrelling while the father still lived, demanding, ‘Divide my share,’ as there is today.

“Aryan doctrine says to direct love toward one's wife after marriage. Why? They married because they needed male children to protect themselves and regarded the wife merely as an instrument for bearing a child. They did not, as Tamils did, marry to find happiness in life or regard a wife as a partner in life.

“A Tamil man did not wish to marry a woman unless he first felt love for her; the same was true of a woman. When a Tamil man first saw a woman, doubt would arise. Why? When two people do not know each other, can love immediately exist? Kambar says that Sita first feels love when she sees Rama in the street and then experiences doubt; Rama likewise experiences doubt. Such doubt is necessary and natural. Valmiki, however, does not say that Rama and Sita even saw one another. Why? Aryans did not have the custom of marrying after first feeling love.

“This doubt arises not only in the man but also in the woman. Yet Nachinarkkiniyar, who came to write a commentary on the *Tolkappiyam*, says, ‘Only a man possesses intelligence.’ The Tamil Sekkizhar, by contrast, says in the *Periyapuranam* that when Sundaramurthi Nayanar and his wife first met, doubt arose in both. That is the superior view. It was the Tamil custom to give women equal rights.

“Tolkappiyar said, ‘Doubt is proper where excellence appears.’ If a man who sees a woman wonders whether she is a prostitute or a chaste woman, there is no room for love to arise there. Inappropriate doubt must not arise. If a man, seeing milk, thinks, ‘Is this cow's milk or pig's milk?’ can he then drink it? Thus love can proceed only where what appears is elevated—that is, where excellence appears. This is what Tolkappiyar meant by ‘doubt where excellence appears.’ Nachinarkkiniyar did not understand it and wrongly said doubt arises only in an excellent man.

“If a daughter loved a man, her parents complied with her wish. Otherwise she would give up her life rather than think of another man; the man was the same. Love therefore had to be equal and mutual on both sides. Only then could life proceed properly. Though lovers made their own decision, they married with parental consent. If consent was withheld, they tried in many ways to obtain it.

“The marriage usually took place in the bride's house. That custom can still be seen in Tamil Nadu. Sometimes, if the woman's parents did not consent, the two left home without anyone's knowledge. When her daughter had gone in this way, the mother worried that the marriage might take place at the bridegroom's home. The family would then search for them, bring them back and solemnise the marriage.

“In those days there were no brokers anywhere—not even between God and people. Even medieval Saivism differed somewhat in this respect. Earlier, a direct relationship existed between God and the living being, with no one in between. Like that relationship, the love of two people was pure.

“Where the text says, ‘The causes involving companions are twelve,’ Nachinarkkiniyar claims there was a Brahmin male companion. That is wrong. Is the Brahmin who comes today to perform a marriage ceremony a companion? Does he not depart after grabbing everything he can obtain?

“The moment the two lovers desired one another, the marriage had effectively occurred. They instituted certain rites merely to make their union known to the world and to remove reproach. That is what *karanam* means. Some of these ancient practices still remain among us.

“Just as the Tamil wished to conduct his household with wealth he had earned through his own effort, extending hospitality—that is, attending to guests—was also one of the Tamil's finest qualities.

“On one occasion a Brahmin elder said, ‘Tamil has no word for “Hospitality”; therefore Tamils never possessed “Hospitality”.’ I immediately looked at him and asked, ‘Have you ever studied Tamil? Have you at least heard the word *velanmai*?’ He stared blankly. ‘All the wealth produced through persevering effort is for the purpose of extending *velanmai* to the worthy...’”

## Part 7

The Tirukkural completes the thought: “...to the worthy, by way of hospitality.” Thus the Tamil lives in order to benefit others. What is renunciation? One who has directed love toward a single family comes to regard the world as one family and direct love toward everyone. That is the duty of a true renunciant. The Tamil does not live for himself but belongs to others. As one Tamil poet said, “This world truly exists because of those who do not live for themselves but belong to others.”

When speaking of love, Tamils did not immerse themselves in bodily—that is, sensual—pleasure. There was no *Kokkokam* among Tamils then. The Tamil did not desire merely physical sensation. No one can alter the words established in a language. If an Englishman says “Warm Reception,” we would have to say “Cool Reception.”

Tamil possesses an eccentricity—a distinctive feature—not found in many languages: only Tamil has the classificatory principle called *uyarthinai*. *Thinai* means conduct. Those who say it means place are ignorant. Inner conduct and outer conduct are what *akathinai* and *purathinai* signify. Conduct is extremely difficult: conduct means not proceeding wherever desire leads. It is possession of self-command. Restraining desire and governing it through reason is firmness, which may also be called steadfastness. I therefore infer that *thinai* may have arisen from *thinmai*, firmness.

In languages such as Sanskrit, every word denoting a woman marks one grammatical gender. Tamil is not like that. Why? Tamil names according to the meaning of the thing. It is said that the human being is elevated because endowed with six faculties of knowledge; to call that *uyarthinai* is nevertheless wrong. Comrade R. Raghava Iyengar further said, “The ability to speak belongs only to the human being; hence the human being is *uyarthinai*.” I reject this too. Are we alone capable of speech? Even an ant speaks. Can we say that ants do not speak merely because we cannot understand them?

Conduct, therefore, is what distinguishes a human being. Possessing conduct means not going wherever desire dictates, but fixing the mind in one course through reason. Human beings alone possess this. Hence that which has elevated conduct became *uyarthinai*. *Ahrinai* does not mean an inferior *thinai*; it means that which has no *thinai*—that is, no conduct: *al* + *thinai* became *ahrinai*.

Someone, however, said, “People, gods and inhabitants of hell are *uyarthinai*.” Tolkappiyar said, “What is called *uyarthinai* denotes people.” All people who possess conduct are *uyarthinai*. Those without it, though human, deserve to be placed in the category of *ahrinai*. In the Kural, “The base resemble people; we have never seen anything else so comparable to them,” those without a sense of conduct are called base. Again, in “The base are like the gods, for they too behave exactly as they please,” the poet says that neither group possesses conduct: both follow wherever the mind leads. Yet a fool calls these gods *uyarthinai*.

Thus from antiquity the Tamil never abandoned conduct. The Tamil principle is that conduct must be guarded more carefully than life. From every perspective, Aryan civilisation and Tamil civilisation differ from one another. The Tamil does not think virtue and pleasure are separate. Tamils are people who, through the love of two persons, uphold virtue and live with happiness.”

Those who hold historical evidence and the words of the learned in mind, discern the subtle truth honourably and with a sense of self-respect, change their personal likes and dislikes and labour without fatigue will certainly find a way both to escape the Aryan illusion themselves and to help others escape it.

Aryanism beautifully declares that everyone is one, only to keep us enslaved. Aryanism remains unwilling to reduce its arrogance and clamour by even an atom, though a thousand years may pass. Aryanism turned kings into mendicants; it enthroned the foolishness that gave houses and estates to those who arrived with sheep and cattle while inflicting harm on everyone else. It nourished fantasy, drove away the intelligence shining in thought, tormented indigenous people and caught Tamils in the device called Brahminism.

That Aryanism will drag God in as its witness! It will recite mantras to intimidate people! It will delude them by calling something Mahadeva's command! It delights in plunging people into limitless stupidity! It was Aryanism that arose across Tamil Nadu like cultivated fields and made the valiant sob. It was Aryanism that turned the martial Tamil who once slapped his shoulders and chest in pride into today's starving commoner, belly drawn in and cheeks hollowed.

Like people whose bodies were ruined by opium and who were then reduced, when it was withheld, to surrendering anything in order to obtain that same drug, Aryanism fed its poison little by little and rendered the Tamil incapable of action. Aryan art destroyed the character of the Tamil people.”

To demonstrate that Aryanism and Tamil are different, I have supplied in this small book the words of researchers. I have also recorded notes on the origin and growth of Aryanism. Is it right for a people possessing such ancient greatness to lie as the bleating goat of another people? Warriors of the Tamil people! Heroes of self-respect! See the technique by which the weak subdued the strong! I call upon you to come forward and labour, making the transformation of this degrading condition the purpose of your lives.

Whether they are ignorant people who ask, “What is an Aryan or a Dravidian?”; arrogant people who say, “What can be done to Aryans?”; or people without courage who declare, “We can accomplish nothing,” I ask whether any of them will come forward to offer a sound and dignified reply to the lessons written here about the early history and growth of the Aryan illusion.

Tamil youth, do not concern yourselves with other people's disposition. Consider patiently and attentively the matters concerning Aryanism that have been gathered and presented here, and reach a conclusion. A fallen people needs spirited youth to rescue it, not useless people with broken ribs!

Heroes born among a people who lived with matchless valour! Can we remain playthings in the hands of whoever picks us up? We descend from Karikala, who engraved the tiger upon the Himalayas. Would the world not laugh to hear that we submit in our hearts to Aryanism, filled with deceit? We have become people without the right even to utter the glory of the Chera, who made the crowns of Kanaka and Vijaya burn, or the name of Rajendra, who ruled with a fearless heart, the waves serving as his ceremonial fans and a ship as his throne. We have become mute, blind and enslaved!

In this land, maidens once ran and played amid gardens, their radiant eyes like sleepless lamps, gold shining on one side and gems on the other; here they themselves shone like gems. Orators once walked this land and skilfully described the splendour in which the flash of a warrior's spear and the smiling arrow of a spear-eyed woman opposed one another. Today, instead, we see an abundance of anguished *venbas* crying, “There is no food, no food!” Towering mansions have become mounds of earth. Because we lost our reason, we lost our wealth and our way. We see the arrows loosed by conspirators piercing our lives. What a condition! Why do our people still fail to perceive the Aryan net?

> Hills of eaglewood and teak that cannot be destroyed,
> seas beautifully heaping up pearls,
> fertile fields where clouds and red paddy resound,
>
> a land fragrant with jasmine forests—

that is our Tamilakam! The jasmine forest now lies as a thorn thicket. Tamilakam must become a flower garden once more. Only on the day our people are freed from the Aryan illusion will they be freed from ignorance, recover courage and become an ornament to the world. Explain that Aryanism is an illusion! See the rare truths supplied by scholars and tell them to others! Begin the righteous struggle! Victory is ours!

❖ ❖ ❖

## Translator's Notes

This canonical work contains the scan-derived continuation, Parts 2–7, of *ஆரிய மாயை / The Aryan Illusion*. The separately inventoried opening is available as [ஆரிய மாயை / The Aryan Illusion](aariyamaayai.md); the two canonical records have not been merged.

Historical, racial and religious generalisations in this work are translated as the author's period polemic or as claims attributed by the author to cited speakers and publications. They have not been silently modernised or independently endorsed.

Printed English passages have been retained as printed, including apparent errors such as “till than,” “from,” “Budhism,” “pannel,” “proable” and “recieved.”

In Somasundara Bharathi's linguistic discussion, *thinai* (திணை) is rendered contextually as a grammatical or ethical “class,” while his proposed connection with *thinmai* (திண்மை, firmness) is preserved as his own inference. *Karanam* (கரணம்) denotes the publicly recognised rites attached here to a marriage; *velanmai* (வேளாண்மை) is used in the cited interpretation in the older sense of beneficent hospitality or service, rather than its usual modern sense of agriculture.

Two tiny scan footers remain non-semantic and uncertain. Their readings and locations are recorded separately in `ocr_concerns.csv`; neither has been used to supply or alter the translated prose.
